```yaml
observation_id: obs_sophist_0001
source_work: Sophist
stephanus_span: 216c-216d
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/sophist.txt
  stephanus_span: 216c-216d
  start_marker: 216c
  end_marker: 216d
  start_char: 785
  end_char: 1429
  text_sha256: 000c8aad961e6a79ca7b3d04c8714234480cfc5e7aab3c1a1c7f0b977256855d
greek_terms:
  - φιλόσοφοι
  - σοφισταί
  - πολιτικοὶ
  - μανικῶς
english_gloss: ""
observation: |
  At the opening of the Sophist, Socrates describes the genuine philosophers (those who are not artificially but truly philosophers) as appearing in multiple guises because of the ignorance of others: they seem to some to be of no worth, to others worthy of everything; they appear now as statesmen (politikoi), now as sophists (sophistai), and sometimes they might even give the impression of being completely mad (manikos).
textual_basis: |
  Socrates enumerates four ways the philosopher appears to others: worthless, worthy of all, a statesman, a sophist, or mad. The enumeration is explicit and follows the comparison of distinguishing the philosophical tribe to distinguishing a god.
limits: |
  The passage does not define the philosopher positively; it only catalogs external misrecognitions. The observation does not claim the philosopher actually is a sophist or statesman.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_sophist_0002

greek_terms:
  - διακρίνειν
  - γένος
  - θεοῦ
english_gloss: ""
observation: |
  Socrates warns that distinguishing the tribe (genos) of the philosopher is hardly easier than distinguishing the tribe of the god, introducing a difficulty hedge at the dialogue's opening before any definitional work begins.
textual_basis: |
  At 216c, Socrates says the genos of the philosopher is not much easier to distinguish (diakrinein) than that of the god, framing the investigation as inherently difficult.
limits: |
  This is a framing remark, not a definitional attempt. It does not propose or reject any specific definiendum.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_sophist_0004
source_work: Sophist
stephanus_span: 216a-216b
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/sophist.txt

  text_sha256: 0060303bac1ecb97583d5577f6c9397d800620ca6ff566b1297e147c54ad3c24
greek_terms:
  []
english_gloss: ""
observation: Theodorus introduces the Eleatic Stranger by civic origin (from Elea), philosophical affiliation (companion of Parmenides and Zeno), and character type (a philosophical man), before the Stranger speaks. The introduction is embedded in a report of a prior agreement (yesterday's agreement) and casts the arrival as a fulfillment of that agreement.
textual_basis: Theodorus names the Stranger's genos (Elea), his hetairos status with the Parmenides-Zeno circle, and calls him mala andra philosophon at 216a. The framing phrase kata tēn chthes homologian marks the encounter as a scheduled meeting, not a chance encounter.
limits: This observation records only the introduction's structure; it does not interpret the Stranger's philosophical views or the significance of Eleatic affiliation beyond what Theodorus states.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_sophist_0005
source_work: Sophist
stephanus_span: 216a-216b
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/sophist.txt
  stephanus_span: 216a-216b
  start_marker: 216a
  end_marker: 216b
  start_char: 0
  end_char: 785
  text_sha256: 0060303bac1ecb97583d5577f6c9397d800620ca6ff566b1297e147c54ad3c24
greek_terms:
  - θεὸν
  - ἐλεγκτικός
english_gloss: ""
observation: Socrates responds to Theodorus's introduction by suggesting that Theodorus may have unknowingly brought a god rather than a stranger, citing Homer on the gods who attend mortals. He specifically likens the Stranger to a 'refutative god' (theos tis elenktikos) who will observe and refute the interlocutors as inferior in argument.
textual_basis: At 216a-b, Socrates asks whether Theodorus has 'unnoticed brought not a stranger but some god' (ou xenon alla tina theon agon ... lelēthas), then quotes Homeric authority about the xenios god who observes hybris and eunomia. He concludes by calling the Stranger a 'refutative god' (theos ōn tis elenktikos) who will oversee and refute 'us who are phauloi in arguments.'
limits: This records Socrates's ironic framing device; it does not claim Socrates actually believes the Stranger is a god, nor does it interpret the irony's target.
review_status: rejected
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_sophist_0006
source_work: Sophist
stephanus_span: 216a-216b
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/sophist.txt

  start_marker: 216a
  end_marker: 216b

  end_char: 785
  text_sha256: 0060303bac1ecb97583d5577f6c9397d800620ca6ff566b1297e147c54ad3c24
greek_terms:
  - θεῖος
english_gloss: ""
observation: "Theodorus corrects Socrates's characterization of the Stranger as a god, denying divinity outright (theos men ... oudamōs) but affirming he is divine (theios mēn). He replaces Socrates's portrait with a measured description: the Stranger is more moderate than those devoted to eristic contests."
textual_basis: "At 216b, Theodorus says ouch houtos ho tropos ... tou xenou, alla metriōteros tōn peri tas eridas espoudakotōn, and explicitly distinguishes theos from theios: kai moi dokei theos men hanēr oudamōs einai, theios mēn."
limits: This records the dramatic correction; it does not assess whether the Stranger's subsequent behavior matches Theodorus's characterization.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml

  - μακρῷ λόγῳ
english_gloss: Socrates asks the Stranger whether he prefers long continuous speech or questioning method.
observation: Socrates opens the conversation by asking the Eleatic Stranger to state his preferred mode of inquiry, giving him a choice between a self-contained long speech and the dialectical method of short question-and-answer.
textual_basis: At 217c Socrates explicitly asks the Stranger whether he is accustomed to prefer a long speech by himself or to proceed through questions. Socrates then offers the Stranger his choice of interlocutor from those present at 217d.
limits: This observation records the initial procedural offer but does not claim that the Stranger accepts or that the dialogue proceeds by the chosen method; those are later developments.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
  - νέος ὤν
  - πρεσβύτου
english_gloss: Socrates mentions his youthful attendance at Parmenides' discourses.
observation: Socrates references his own past attendance at Parmenides when the latter conducted magnificent discourses through questioning, framing the present encounter with the Eleatic Stranger against that earlier experience.
textual_basis: At 217c Socrates says that he himself once witnessed Parmenides using the method of questioning and going through fine arguments when Socrates was young and Parmenides quite old. This personal report embeds the present inquiry in a prior philosophical encounter.
limits: The observation records only the frame reported by Socrates; it does not address whether the Stranger is a Parmenidean follower or the content of those earlier discourses.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_sophist_0009
source_work: Sophist
stephanus_span: 217c-217d
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/sophist.txt
  stephanus_span: 217c-217d
  start_marker: 217c
  end_marker: 217d
  start_char: 2262
  end_char: 3036
  text_sha256: 0ead71aeb239a9a9e3abd4048a9cfa8647f77994bb3f73bcfde4c41ab4a571ec
greek_terms:
  - αἰδώς
  - πρῶτον συγγενόμενον
english_gloss: The Stranger expresses hesitation about switching to short exchange on first meeting.
observation: "The Eleatic Stranger responds to Socrates' procedural offer by stating that he feels a certain shame or scruple about engaging in word-by-word exchange immediately, since this is his first meeting with the company. He distinguishes between two modes: conversing with someone who is compliant and gentle makes the other-directed mode easier, while otherwise he would prefer to speak by himself."
textual_basis: At 217d the Stranger says that with someone who converses without pain and gently it is easier to proceed with another, but otherwise he would do it by himself. He then adds that he feels a sense of hesitation about doing brief exchange on first meeting. This establishes a procedural reluctance before the dialogue's chosen method is set.
limits: The observation records the Stranger's initial procedural hesitation. It does not claim that the hesitation is resolved or that the dialogue ultimately abandons the question-and-answer form; the sequel shows that the procedure is in fact adopted.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_sophist_0010
source_work: Sophist
stephanus_span: 217a-217b
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/sophist.txt
  stephanus_span: 217a-217b
  start_marker: 217a
  end_marker: 217b
  start_char: 1429
  end_char: 2262
  text_sha256: 05f3dd944c3b42747e265f614973df28ecfc7a82b59fae1e2cb8486f25154e46
greek_terms:
  - σοφιστήν
  - πολιτικόν
  - φιλόσοφον
english_gloss: sophist, statesman, philosopher
observation: Socrates asks the Eleatic stranger how people in his region classify and name sophist, statesman, and philosopher — whether as one, two, or three distinct kinds — and whether they assign a separate genos to each name. The stranger confirms three are distinguished but adding that giving a clear definition of each is a substantial task.
textual_basis: At 217a-217b Socrates asks whether the Eleatic countrymen held sophist, statesman, and philosopher to be one, two, or three kinds (three names, three kinds). The stranger confirms three and notes the difficulty of defining each clearly.
limits: The passage sets up the inquiry but does not itself produce any definitions. It signals that classification and definition will be the programme without delivering the content.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_sophist_0013
source_work: Sophist
stephanus_span: 217e-218a
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/sophist.txt

greek_terms:
  - προσδιαλεγόμενον
english_gloss: ""
observation: The Eleatic Stranger accepts Theodorus's request to proceed by question-and-answer with Theaetetus as respondent, after first noting that a long display speech (epideixis) would be an alternative format but that refusing the request would appear unfriendly. Theaetetus then confirms the arrangement.
textual_basis: At 217e the Stranger describes the epideixis format as an option, then rejects it, accepts Theaetetus as interlocutor, and Theaetetus at 218a urges the Stranger to proceed as Socrates proposed. The Stranger then addresses Theaetetus directly, formally starting the inquiry.
limits: This observation records the procedural turn-format choice; it does not address the content of the inquiry about the sophist, statesman, and philosopher.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
textual_basis: At 218a the Stranger pre-assigns responsibility for the dialogue's potential length, telling Theaetetus not to blame him but to blame Theaetetus's companions if the length causes trouble.
```

```yaml
observation: Theaetetus offers to summon his namesake 'Socrates the Younger,' described as his age-mate and co-gymnast, as a substitute respondent if he himself should falter during the inquiry.
textual_basis: "At 218b Theaetetus says: 'we shall take Socrates, the namesake of Socrates, my age-mate and co-gymnast' — ton Sōkratē ton Sōkratous men homōnymon, emon de hēlikiōtēn kai syngymnastēn. The introduction identifies the character by patronymic echo and by social-habitus markers (age-mate, co-gymnast)."
limits: This observation records the second named Socrates introduced at the dialogue's opening and the dramatic provision for a substitute interlocutor. It does not claim that the substitution is used or that the figure's identity carries any further claim.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_sophist_0018
source_work: Sophist
stephanus_span: 218b-218c
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/sophist.txt
  stephanus_span: 218b-218c
  start_marker: 218b
  end_marker: 218c
  start_char: 3819
  end_char: 4667
  text_sha256: c713f9dda23d6631005320543ef3786d74abc7cfdc543d442f21d89ae8770078
greek_terms:
  []
english_gloss: ""
observation: The stranger distinguishes sharing only a name from sharing an account of the thing itself and insists that it is necessary to agree about the matter itself through argument rather than merely the name without argument — making a shared logoi-based account the criterion for joint investigation.
textual_basis: At 218c the stranger contrasts 'the name only' (tounoma monon) held in common with the need 'to always agree about the thing itself through arguments rather than the name only without argument' — dei de aei pantos peri to pragma auto mallon dia logōn ē tounoma monon synōmologēsthai chōris logou. This explicitly distinguishes an onoma-level agreement from a logōn-level account and sets the requirement for dialectical investigation.
limits: This observation highlights the methodological principle announced at the dialogue's opening. It does not claim that the principle is successfully applied or that it is unique to the Sophist.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml

greek_terms:
  - προμελετᾶν
  - συμβουλεύω
english_gloss: ""
observation: At 218d-218e the Eleatic Stranger proposes a methodological procedure — first practice on something small and easy before tackling the main difficult genus — and Theaetetus assents without hesitation to the proposed approach.
textual_basis: The Stranger explicitly advises approaching the difficult sophist genus by first practicing method on something easier (218d), frames it as a recommendation, and proposes a paradigm of the angler as a preliminary target (218e). Theaetetus gives three short assents across 218d-218e, agreeing he has no alternative route, accepting the proposal, and affirming the angler as suitable.
limits: This observation records only the procedural framing and assent; it does not evaluate whether the angler example succeeds as a paradigm or how the method is executed later.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_sophist_0020

  - ἀσπαλιευτής
  - παράδειγμα
english_gloss: ""
observation: At 218e the Stranger proposes the angler as a preliminary paradigm for practicing the method of division before applying it to the sophist, selecting it explicitly because it is small, easily recognizable, and unworthy of much serious attention.
textual_basis: The Stranger asks what to set before them as something easily recognizable, small, yet having an account no lesser than greater things (218e); he then proposes the angler as universally familiar and not meriting much serious effort. This frames a humble craft as a methodological stepping-stone to the larger target.
limits: This observation concerns only the selection and characterization of the angler paradigm at 218e. It does not address how the subsequent division of the angler unfolds or whether the paradigm succeeds.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
textual_basis: The Stranger characterizes the sophist genus as difficult and hard to hunt (218d) and proposes practicing method on something easier first. The hunting metaphor frames the sophist as prey to be captured through division, and the genus language marks it as a classificatory target.
limits: This observation records only the characterization of the sophist as a difficult definiendum at 218d. It does not assess whether the subsequent divisions succeed in capturing it.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
  - ἀρχώμεθα
  - τεχνίτην
  - ἄτεχνον
  - καλῶς ἂν ἔχοι
english_gloss: ""
observation: The Stranger obtains procedural assent to begin and immediately poses the first alternative about whether the target has a craft.
textual_basis: At 219a Theaetetus accepts the proposed method before answering the Stranger's first craft-or-artless question.
limits: This records the inquiry setup and first turn structure, not the substantive productive-craft definition.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation: "The Stranger establishes the definition of poietike through a chain of short assents from Theaetetus at 219b: the criterion (bringing into ousia) is stated, assented to (orthos), applied to the previously listed technai, and then named (poietiken prosipomen)."
textual_basis: At 219b Theaetetus assents to the criterion (orthos), then assents that the previously enumerated technai have their power (dunamin) directed toward this (eiche gar oun), after which the Stranger names the kind poietike. The pattern is criterion-application-naming.
limits: This observation covers only the first half of the initial division; the second kind (ktetike) is not yet introduced in this span. The assent chain is short and does not involve resistance.
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_sophist_0025
source_work: Sophist
stephanus_span: 219e-220a
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/sophist.txt

  text_sha256: 33edaf4a0914d7d85c5c06f7f3a877b93227488222dc378e05accbc260be77f8
greek_terms:
  []
english_gloss: ""
observation: |
  The Eleatic Stranger divides the acquisitive art by an explicit dichotomous procedure: one part is openly competitive, the other is secretly done and wholly hunting. The hunting half is then divided by object: soulless versus ensouled prey. The latter is named animal-hunting, which is further divided into land-hunting and water-hunting.
textual_basis: |
  At 219e-220a the Stranger proposes successive binary cuts: open vs. hidden of the agonistic half (219e); then soulless vs. ensouled within the hunting half (219e-220a); then land vs. swimming within animal-hunting (220a). Each cut produces a named or unnamed subclass.
limits: |
  This observation records the structure of dichotomous division as it occurs in 219e-220a. It does not assess whether the division is exhaustive or whether the unnamed branch (soulless) would be filled in later.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
textual_basis: |
  Theaetetus's turns at 219e-220a are: yes (219e); say how (219e); what else? if indeed both exist (219e-220a); let it be so (220a); certainly (220a). None offers resistance or substantive contribution to the divisions.
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_sophist_0028
source_work: Sophist
stephanus_span: 220d-221a
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/sophist.txt

  start_char: 8481

  text_sha256: 3b380e3ef055e77f6bf39e41f9140f2159de4ebecc7352861acec78494cc135f
greek_terms:
  - ἀγκιστρευτικόν
  - τριοδοντία
  - ἀσπαλιευτικῆς
english_gloss: ""
observation: |
  The Eleatic Stranger performs a successive dichotomous division of striking-hunting at 220d-221a: first by time of day into night hunting by fire-light and day hunting with hooks and tridents, then by direction and tool into downward trident-fishing and upward hook-fishing. Each cut produces named sub-kinds.
textual_basis: |
  At 220d the Stranger introduces striking-hunting as a single kind, then divides it into night hunting called pyreutike and day hunting called ankistreutikon. At 220e-221a ankistreutikon is further divided into triodontia, which strikes from above downward, and the remaining hook-fishing kind, which draws from below upward around the head and mouth. The passage is a source-backed series of twofold divisions within the angler example.
limits: |
  This observation notes the formal structure of the division without assessing its correctness, completeness, or relationship to definitions of the sophist elsewhere. It does not claim this is the only method of division used in the dialogue.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
textual_basis: |
  Theaetetus's responses are: "pany ge" (220d, after the fire-hunting naming), "legetai gar oun" (220e, after the day-angling classification), and "phasi goun tines" (220e, after the trident-fishing naming). Each is a brief formula confirming the Stranger's point without adding new content or resisting the division.
limits: |
  This observation notes the assent pattern in this segment only. It does not claim Theaetetus is passive throughout the dialogue or that minimal assent always indicates agreement rather than procedural cooperation.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_sophist_0030

stephanus_span: 220d-220e

greek_terms:
  - ἀγκιστρευτικόν
  - τριοδοντία
  - ἀμελῶμεν τοῦ ὀνόματος
english_gloss: ""
observation: |
  The Stranger names each sub-kind produced by the division of striking-hunting after its characteristic operation or tool: fire-hunting (220d), angling (220d), and trident-fishing (220e). Theaetetus explicitly accepts provisional, operational names at 220d, saying not to worry about the name since this one suffices.
textual_basis: |
  At 220d the Stranger names night hunting by fire "pyreutike" (from pyr, fire) and day hunting with hooks and tridents "ankistreutikon" (from ankistron, hook). At 220e he names the downward trident method "triodontia" (from triodous, trident). Theaetetus had already conceded at 220d: "amelōmen tou onomatos; arkei gar kai touto" (let us not worry about the name; this one suffices).
limits: |
  This observation records the pattern of operational naming and the respondent's concession about nomenclature. It does not assess whether these names are conventional or newly coined, nor does it claim this naming pattern is unique to this dialogue.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
  The Stranger explicitly states the winged/water-dwelling division at 220b, names bird-hunting and fishing as the corresponding techniques, then at 220b-c introduces the dichotomy of hunting by enclosures versus by blow as the largest parts of water-dwelling hunting, coining the term enclosure-hunting at 220c.
limits: |
```

```yaml

textual_basis: At 222b the Stranger states that there are two largest parts of terrestrial hunting (tame and wild). When Theaetetus asks whether there is a hunt of tame animals, the Stranger at 222b-c offers a range of alternative positions Theaetetus might hold about humans as tame or wild and whether there is a hunt of humans, concluding with an instruction to define whichever option he prefers.
limits: This observation notes the branching structure but does not evaluate whether the alternatives are exhaustive or whether the Stranger is steering Theaetetus toward a specific branch.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
limits: This observation records the metaphorical shift within the diairetic frame. It does not claim the division is invalidated by the metaphor, nor does it analyze whether this is a deliberate signal about the limits of the diairetic method.
```

```yaml

observation: "The Stranger frames the initial inquiry about the sophist as a forced alternative: either he is a private person (idiotes) or he possesses a craft (techne), at 221c-d."
textual_basis: "The Stranger recalls the first question about the angler — 'whether we should posit the angler as a private person or as someone having a craft' (221c) — and then applies it to the sophist: 'Shall we now posit this man as a private person, Theaetetus, or altogether truly a sophist?' (221d). Theaetetus answers he is no idiotes, and the Stranger concludes 'we must posit him as having some craft' (221d)."
limits: The forced alternative is binary and preliminary; it does not prove the sophist has a genuine craft, only that the inquiry proceeds on that assumption.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
  At 221b the Stranger recapitulates the complete chain of dichotomous divisions that produced the definition of the angler, tracing the path from the whole of art through successive bifurcations: acquisitive, subduing, hunting, animal-hunting, water-hunting, fishing, striking, and finally hooking.
textual_basis: |
  The Stranger lays out the chain with repeated genitives and definite articles marking each bifurcation, tracing the division from the whole of art through each successive half, demonstrating the method of definition by successive dichotomous division from a broad genus down to the specific form.
limits: |
  This is the recapitulation of a division already performed earlier in the dialogue; it does not represent a new division. The observation records the method as displayed in the summary, not the full defense of dichotomous division as a definitional method.
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_sophist_0043
source_work: Sophist

greek_terms:
  - πιθανουργικῆς
  - ἰδιοθηρευτικῆς
  - μισθαρνητικόν
  - δωροφορικόν
english_gloss: Subdivision of persuasion-production into two kinds, then subdivision of the private-hunting kind into wage-earning and gift-bearing.
observation: At 222d-222e the Eleatic Stranger continues the diairetic division of the sophist by bifurcating the art of persuasion-production into two kinds (private / public), then immediately subdividing the private-hunting kind into a wage-earning branch and a gift-bearing branch. The division proceeds by successive binary cuts without deductive justification.
textual_basis: At 222d the Stranger says "Of the art of persuasion-production let us say there are two kinds" and Theaetetus asks what they are; the Stranger answers "One occurring in private, the other in public." Theaetetus assents. Then at 222d the Stranger asks "And again of the private-hunting kind, isn't one part wage-earning and the other gift-bearing?" Theaetetus says he doesn't understand, leading to a brief clarifying exchange (222d-222e) before the Stranger assigns the gift-bearing part to the erotic art at 222e.
limits: The observation notes the formal pattern of dichotomous division; it does not assess whether the divisions correctly capture the sophist's nature or whether they are exhaustive. It does not evaluate the erotic digression that follows.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_sophist_0044
source_work: Sophist
stephanus_span: 222d-222e
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/sophist.txt
  stephanus_span: 222d-222e
  start_marker: 222d
  end_marker: 222e
  start_char: 12419
  end_char: 13090
  text_sha256: 7c986e3c8e19ab599d700f8c7f5725030baaedb1235754b312d1a7c40a55c5c7
greek_terms:
  - τῇ τῶν ἐρώντων θήρᾳ
  - ἐρωτικῆς τέχνης
english_gloss: The Stranger introduces the hunt of lovers and assigns the gift-bearing branch to the erotic art.
observation: "At 222d the Stranger explains the gift-bearing branch of private hunting by invoking the hunt of lovers: lovers give gifts to those they have hunted. He then at 222e formally assigns this gift-bearing kind to the erotic art. The hunting analogy used throughout the sophist division is here applied directly to erotic pursuit."
textual_basis: Theaetetus says he doesn't understand the wage-earning / gift-bearing distinction (222d). The Stranger replies "You haven't yet paid attention, it seems, to the hunt of lovers — that they give gifts in addition to those they have hunted" (222d-222e). Theaetetus assents, and the Stranger concludes "Then let this be a kind of the erotic art" (222e).
limits: This observation notes the mapping of hunting vocabulary onto erotic behavior within a diairetic context. It does not claim that this is a substantive theory of eros, nor does it assess whether the analogy is adequate. The erotic digression is brief and is used to clarify a branch of the division, not to analyze eros independently.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_sophist_0046
source_work: Sophist
stephanus_span: 223c-223d
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/sophist.txt
  stephanus_span: 223c-223d
  start_marker: 223c
  end_marker: 223d
  start_char: 13963
  end_char: 14826
  text_sha256: 6a1c5bcc115beafbddbd5379580d9d6bd959c3bab4077f25db285959c932bb08
greek_terms:
  []
english_gloss: ""
observation: "Theaetetus responds to each of the Stranger's divisional moves with brief assents: 'pēi dē?' (how?), 'ēn gar oun' (yes it was), 'eirēsthō' (let it be stated), 'panu ge' (certainly), 'nai' (yes), and 'ti d' ou?' (why not?). The Stranger drives the division tree while Theaetetus only asks one brief clarifying question and otherwise concedes every step."
textual_basis: Throughout 223c-223d Theaetetus gives only short responses. At 223c after the Stranger's opening warning he asks 'pēi dē?' and then concedes the prior division with 'ēn gar oun'. At 223d he responds 'panu ge', 'nai', and 'ti d' ou?' to successive subdivisions without challenging or extending any branch.
limits: This observation records the turn pattern in this passage; it does not claim that Theaetetus fails to follow the argument or that the assents are problematic.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_sophist_0047
source_work: Sophist
stephanus_span: 223c-223d
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/sophist.txt
  stephanus_span: 223c-223d
  start_marker: 223c
  end_marker: 223d
  start_char: 13963
  end_char: 14826
  text_sha256: 6a1c5bcc115beafbddbd5379580d9d6bd959c3bab4077f25db285959c932bb08
greek_terms:
  []
english_gloss: ""
observation: "The Eleatic Stranger recapitulates and extends a dichotomous division of the acquisitive art: ktetike is split into hunting (thēreutikon) and exchange (allaktikon); allaktike is split into gift-giving (dōrētikon) and commerce (agorastikon); agorastikē is split into direct-sale of own goods (autopōlikē) and resale of others' goods (metablētikē); metablētikē is split into city-retail (kapēlikē) and intercity merchant trade (emporikē). Each branch is named by coining or deploying a technical term."
textual_basis: At 223c the Stranger recalls ktetike art as twofold with thēreutikon and allaktikon. He then divides allaktikē into dōrētikon and agorastikon. At 223d he further divides agorastikē into autopōlikē and metablētikē, and divides metablētikē into kapēlikē (roughly half, he says) and emporikē (buying and selling between cities).
limits: This observation records the structure of the division and the coining of technical names; it does not evaluate whether the divisions are exhaustive or mutually exclusive, nor whether the sophist is captured by any branch.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml

greek_terms:
  - ἐμπορικῆς
  - σῶμα
  - ψυχή
english_gloss: commerce, body, soul
observation: The Eleatic Stranger divides commerce (emporike) into two kinds based on whether the goods traded nourish the body or the soul.
textual_basis: At 223e the Stranger asks whether they have noticed that one kind of commerce exchanges things that nourish and serve the body, while another exchanges things for the soul. Theaetetus confirms understanding of the body-commerce but acknowledges ignorance of the soul-commerce.
limits: This observation records only the body/soul bifurcation within the commerce branch of the ongoing diairesis. It does not address where this division leads in subsequent passages.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
  - σπουδῆς
english_gloss: music, painting, wonder-working, consolation, serious purpose
observation: "At 224a the soul-commerce branch is populated with a catalogue of items: music (mousike), painting (graphike), wonder-working (thaumatopoiike), and many other things for the soul, some brought for consolation (paramythia) and some for serious purposes (spoude)."
textual_basis: The Stranger enumerates mousike, graphike, thaumatopoiike, and many other things of the soul at 224a, adding that they are brought and sold either for consolation or for serious pursuits.
limits: This records the catalogue as a step in the diairetic hunt for the sophist. It does not claim that these items exhaust the domain or that the sophist is identified with any specific one.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
textual_basis: At 224a the Stranger states that the person bringing and selling these soul-items is correctly called an emporos no less than the seller of food and drink, asserting parity between body-commerce and soul-commerce merchants.
```

```yaml
textual_basis: At 225c the Stranger states that the artless kind must be posited as an eidos because the logos has recognized it as heteron on, explicitly using eidos-vocabulary for a class arrived at through division.
limits: This does not entail any commitment to transcendent forms; the passage uses eidos in the context of a diairetic classification.
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_sophist_0052
source_work: Sophist
stephanus_span: 225a-225b
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/sophist.txt
  stephanus_span: 225a-225b
  start_marker: 225a
  end_marker: 225b
  start_char: 16970
  end_char: 17830
  text_sha256: 2bd8a8d01c9c03cb1e7a33814683f7973dffa3fa4b64be24335c40327efeaa7d
greek_terms:
  - ἁμιλλητικὸν
  - μαχητικόν
  - βιαστικόν
  - ἀμφισβητητικόν
english_gloss: ""
observation: |
  At 225a-b the Eleatic Stranger divides the agonistic part of acquisition (ktêtikê) by successive dichotomies. Within the agonistic, he splits competition (hamillêtikon) from combat (machêtikon). Combat is then divided into violent bodily combat (biastikon) and disputation by speeches (amphisbêtêtikon). Amphisbêtêtikon is further divided by whether it concerns public lengthy speeches about justice and injustice (dikanikon, forensic) or private exchanges broken into short question-and-answer (antilogikon).
textual_basis: |
  The passage (225a-b) proceeds through a chain of explicit binary divisions marked by men/de and to/tode pairs: hamillêtikon vs. machêtikon, biastikon vs. amphisbêtêtikon, dikanikon vs. antilogikon. Each division receives Theaetetus's assent.
limits: |
  This observation records the diairetic structure but does not evaluate whether the divisions are exhaustive or whether the Stranger will later revise any branches.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
limits: This records the turn pattern; it does not treat the passage's division vocabulary as a self-reference to dialogue form.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_sophist_0054
source_work: Sophist
stephanus_span: 225a-225b
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/sophist.txt
  stephanus_span: 225a-225b
  start_marker: 225a
  end_marker: 225b
  start_char: 16970
  end_char: 17830
  text_sha256: 2bd8a8d01c9c03cb1e7a33814683f7973dffa3fa4b64be24335c40327efeaa7d
greek_terms:
  - μήκεσί τε πρὸς ἐναντία μήκη λόγων
  - ἐν ἰδίοις
english_gloss: ""
observation: |
  The Stranger's division at 225a-b shifts from a physical domain (bodies fighting bodies, biastikon) to a speech domain (logoi against logoi, amphisbêtêtikon). Within the speech domain, a further shift distinguishes the public and forensic (dikanikon) from the private and fragmented (antilogikon). This embeds the distinction between long public speeches and short private question-and-answer within the diairetic structure.
textual_basis: |
  The division moves explicitly from body-to-body combat (sômati pros sômata, 225a) to speech-to-speech (logois pros logous, 225a-b). Within the speech branch, the Stranger distinguishes dêmosia long speeches (mêkesi te pros enantia mêkê logôn, 225b) from private (en idiois, 225b) fragmented question-and-answer.
limits: |
  This observation records the domain-shift structure within the division. It does not claim that the Stranger assigns evaluative priority to either domain or that the physical/speech distinction maps onto any metaphysical distinction.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_sophist_0055
source_work: Sophist
stephanus_span: 224b-224c
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/sophist.txt
  stephanus_span: 224b-224c
  start_marker: 224b
  end_marker: 224c
  start_char: 15485
  end_char: 16302
  text_sha256: e283b446657486afd1c6d38032ab7af7ec062f4929e9552ccc02951f5f7e3f11
greek_terms:
  - ψυχεμπορικῆς
  - μαθηματοπωλικῆς
  - σοφιστικὸν γένος
english_gloss: soul-merchandising, learning-selling, craft-selling, the sophistic kind
observation: At 224b-224c the Eleatic Stranger continues the diairetic division of the sophist by distinguishing sub-branches of acquisition. The soul-merchandising branch is split into display and a second branch that sells learnings. This second branch is then divided into craft-selling, covering learnings of the other crafts, and a remaining branch which Theaetetus identifies as the sophistic kind.
textual_basis: The Stranger at 224b introduces the division within soul-merchandising, calling one part display and requiring another name for the part that sells learnings. At 224c he further divides the learning-selling branch into craft-selling for the crafts and asks Theaetetus to name the remainder, which Theaetetus supplies as the sophistic kind.
limits: This observation records the formal dichotomous division step within the on-going diairesis but does not assess whether the division is sound or captures the essence of the sophist.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_sophist_0056
source_work: Sophist
stephanus_span: 225e-226a
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/sophist.txt
  stephanus_span: 225e-226a
  start_marker: 225e
  end_marker: 226a
  start_char: 18693
  end_char: 19320
  text_sha256: 6b48d7c492235984aa0ff9ef541c5dd089f6e45bfacadb79dea29d0bceb621aa
greek_terms:
  []
english_gloss: ""
observation: At 225e the Eleatic Stranger instructs Theaetetus to name the opposite kind — the one who earns money from private disputes — which Theaetetus identifies as the sophist appearing for a fourth time. At 226a the Stranger consolidates this by enumerating a chain of sub-kinds (acquisitive genus, belonging to the art of disputation, of contradiction, of contention, of combat, of contest) that the account has now revealed as the sophist.
textual_basis: The Stranger explicitly frames this as the contrary (tounantion) of the previous kind, and Theaetetus responds that no other answer would be correct except that the marvellous sophist has come round again, now a fourth time. The Stranger then recapitulates the chain of genos-species relations that the method has disclosed.
limits: This observation records the diairetic enumeration and the count of appearances (fourth). It does not evaluate whether the division captures sophistry adequately or whether the chain is logically sound.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
textual_basis: At 225e-226a the animal and capture language directly frames the sophist as difficult prey in the ongoing hunting analogy.
limits: This is a hunting image for the sophist, not a claim about psychology or animal nature in general.
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_sophist_0058
source_work: Sophist
stephanus_span: 225e-226a

greek_terms:
  []
english_gloss: ""
observation: At 225e Theaetetus responds to the Stranger's request that he name the opposite kind by immediately providing the required answer without resistance, and at 226a he gives unqualified assent ('Certainly') to the Stranger's recapitulation, then again affirms the method when asked whether both hands are needed to catch the sophist.
textual_basis: "Theaetetus's three responses in 225e-226a consist of: a cooperative attempt to name the kind, a strong affirmative (komide men oun), and a procedural acknowledgment (oukoun amphoin chre). No challenge, hesitation, or deflection occurs."
limits: This records the assent pattern as a turn-geometry feature of cooperative inquiry. It does not claim that Theaetetus's assent indicates independent verification or that the division is correct.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_sophist_0059
source_work: Sophist
stephanus_span: 227c-227d
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/sophist.txt
  stephanus_span: 227c-227d
  start_marker: 227c
  end_marker: 227d
  start_char: 21719
  end_char: 22630
  text_sha256: 5ca01daa756b81e95831f601f676e61b48a700a3645a0a951651acc27158fa49
greek_terms:
  - καθάρσεως
  - εἴδη
  - ψυχὴν
  - σῶμα
english_gloss: ""
observation: |
  The Visitor separates purification (katharsis) into two kinds (eide): purification concerned with the soul and purification concerned with the body, counted as distinct from each other. Theaetetus assents to this division at 227c.
textual_basis: |
  At 227c the Visitor states that the present inquiry has undertaken to mark off purification concerning thought (dianoia) from the rest. Theaetetus then concedes two eide of purification, with the soul-kind separate from the body-kind (227c). The Visitor immediately moves to cut the soul-kind further dichotomously at 227d, asking Theaetetus to attempt to divide what has been said in two.
limits: |
  The observation records the procedural act of dichotomous division at this stage; it does not track whether the division is ultimately adequate or revised later.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_sophist_0060
source_work: Sophist
stephanus_span: 227c-227d
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/sophist.txt
  stephanus_span: 227c-227d
  start_marker: 227c
  end_marker: 227d
  start_char: 21719
  end_char: 22630
  text_sha256: 5ca01daa756b81e95831f601f676e61b48a700a3645a0a951651acc27158fa49
greek_terms:
  - πονηρίαν
  - ἀρετῆς
  - κακίας
  - καθαρμός
english_gloss: ""
observation: |
  The Visitor consolidates the account of purification by linking it to the removal of vice from the soul: wickedness (poneria) is introduced as something other than virtue in the soul (227d), purification is restated as leaving the better and expelling whatever is base, and purification of the soul is then defined as the removal of vice (kakia) insofar as it is found there.
textual_basis: |
  At 227d the Visitor asks whether wickedness (poneria) is something other than virtue in the soul, obtains assent, then recalls that purification was defined as leaving one thing and expelling whatever is base. He then concludes that, insofar as they find some removal of vice concerning the soul, they will properly call that purification of the soul. Theaetetus assents.
limits: |
  This records the consolidation of a definition chain at this point in the diairetic procedure; it does not claim finality.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml

  - διχῇ τέμνειν
  - συντέμνειν
english_gloss: ""
observation: |
  At 227d the Visitor directs Theaetetus to follow along and attempt to cut the stated point dichotomously, and Theaetetus agrees to try to cut along with him according to his guidance.
textual_basis: |
  The Visitor says at 227d: "and next attend, trying again to cut what has been said in two" (epakoue piromenos au to lechthen dichē temnein). Theaetetus responds: "In whatever way you lead, I shall try to cut along with you" (kath' hopoi' an huphēgēi peirasomai soi suntemnein).
limits: |
  This records a procedural agreement to continue the diairetic method together; it does not evaluate the content of the division.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_sophist_0062
source_work: Sophist
stephanus_span: 227a-227b
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/sophist.txt
  stephanus_span: 227a-227b
  start_marker: 227a
  end_marker: 227b
  start_char: 20850
  end_char: 21719
  text_sha256: 13be6ed0327801be6c05aa4324e545eb545f079fbb7cd13e66a39ebb791a67d3
greek_terms:
  - τὸ συγγενὲς
  - τὸ μὴ συγγενὲς
  - νοῦν
english_gloss: the kindred, the unkindred, understanding
observation: |
  The Eleatic Stranger describes the purpose of the method of logoi as acquiring understanding by discerning what is kindred and what is not kindred among all crafts, treating them all equally for this purpose (227b). This announces the diairetic method's core operation of dividing kinds by kinship.
textual_basis: |
  At 227b the Stranger states the method aims at acquiring understanding through observing the kindred and unkindred across all crafts, and that it honors all crafts equally for this end. The kinship/non-kinship pair is the conceptual engine of the dichotomous division procedure central to the Sophist.
limits: |
  This record notes the statement of method but does not evaluate whether the subsequent divisions adhere to it, nor does it import later refinements of the method from other parts of the dialogue.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_sophist_0063
source_work: Sophist
stephanus_span: 228a-228b
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/sophist.txt
  stephanus_span: 228a-228b
  start_marker: 228a
  end_marker: 228b
  start_char: 22630
  end_char: 23348
  text_sha256: a654a6233c702f7645231dacf12a590be141f12e27b25471f045d3fe48028156
greek_terms:
  - νόσον
  - αἶσχος
  - στάσιν
  - πονηρίαν
english_gloss: disease, ugliness/deformity, faction/civil war, wickedness
observation: "The Eleatic Stranger uses bodily evil as an analogue for psychic evil: he distinguishes disease and ugliness in the body, defines faction as discord among what is naturally akin, and then names faction and disease of the soul as wickedness."
textual_basis: At 228a the Stranger distinguishes two bodily defects, one like disease and one like ugliness. He then defines *stasis* as discord of the naturally akin and *aischos* as disproportion. At 228b, after listing psychic conflicts among opinions, desires, spirit, pleasures, reason, and pains, he concludes that calling faction and disease of the soul wickedness is correct.
limits: This observation notes the body-soul analogy in this local argument. It does not claim a one-to-one mapping of every bodily defect onto every psychic defect, nor does it import later psychology from other dialogues.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_sophist_0064
source_work: Sophist
stephanus_span: 228a-228b
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/sophist.txt
  stephanus_span: 228a-228b
  start_marker: 228a
  end_marker: 228b
  start_char: 22630
  end_char: 23348
  text_sha256: a654a6233c702f7645231dacf12a590be141f12e27b25471f045d3fe48028156
greek_terms:
  - συγγενοῦς
  - διαφθορᾶς
  - διαφοράν
english_gloss: akin/congenital, corruption, difference/discord
observation: The Stranger provides a definition of *stasis* (faction) as 'the discord of the naturally akin arising from some corruption' and secures Theaetetus's assent. He then defines *aischos* (ugliness) as 'the everywhere misshapen genus of disproportion that is present.' Both definitions are established through short question-and-assent steps.
textual_basis: "At 228a, the Stranger asks: 'Do you consider *stasis* to be anything other than the discord of what is naturally akin arising from some corruption?' Theaetetus answers 'nothing else.' At 228a-b, the Stranger asks: 'But is ugliness anything other than the everywhere misshapen genus of disproportion that is present?' Theaetetus answers: 'In no way anything else.' Both definitions are secured by the interlocutor's assent."
limits: This observation records two definitions posited and assented to; it does not assess whether the definitions survive later examination or whether Theaetetus's minimal assent indicates genuine understanding.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_sophist_0065
source_work: Sophist
stephanus_span: 228a-228b

  source_path: raw/plato/greek/sophist.txt

  start_marker: 228a
  end_marker: 228b
  start_char: 22630
  end_char: 23348
  text_sha256: a654a6233c702f7645231dacf12a590be141f12e27b25471f045d3fe48028156
greek_terms:
  - δόξας
  - ἐπιθυμίαις
  - θυμὸν
  - ἡδοναῖς
  - λόγον
  - λύπαις
english_gloss: opinions, desires, spirit, pleasures, reason, pains
observation: "The Stranger catalogues internal psychic conflicts observed in base people: opinions at war with desires, spirit with pleasures, reason with pains, and all these elements in mutual conflict with one another. Theaetetus strongly affirms this observation."
textual_basis: "At 228b the Stranger asks: 'And what? In the soul, have we not perceived opinions at variance with desires, and spirit with pleasures, and reason with pains, and all these things at variance with one another in those who are in a base condition?' Theaetetus answers 'Very much so indeed.' The Stranger then notes that all these elements are necessarily akin (*sungene*), laying the ground for calling their discord a *stasis* of the soul."
limits: This observation notes the claim of internal conflict; it does not evaluate whether the tripartite psychology implied here (opinions/desires, spirit/pleasures, reason/pains) maps onto the tripartition of the soul in Republic IV or whether the Stranger's classification exhausts psychic conflict types.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_sophist_0066
source_work: Sophist
stephanus_span: 228c-228d
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/sophist.txt
  stephanus_span: 228c-228d
  start_marker: 228c
  end_marker: 228d
  start_char: 23348
  end_char: 24153
  text_sha256: ca71b4f4cef5e6146f5321e6dffe1d7746533860257f9a74575c78bb8b8c952b
greek_terms:
  - συμμετρίας
  - ἀμετρίας
english_gloss: symmetry, lack of symmetry
observation: "The Eleatic Stranger frames the analysis of psychic failure as a forced binary: a moving thing that aims at a target and misses does so either through symmetry or through asymmetry. Theaetetus is compelled to choose the second horn."
textual_basis: At 228c the Stranger asks whether beings that share in motion and set a target but miss it every time they attempt it suffer this through symmetry toward one another or, on the contrary, through asymmetry. Theaetetus answers clearly through asymmetry. The question form eliminates middle options.
limits: This observation records the binary structure of the question, not whether the dichotomy is philosophically sound. It does not address whether the Stranger's subsequent application of this dichotomy to the soul is valid.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_sophist_0067
source_work: Sophist
stephanus_span: 228c-228d

  source_path: raw/plato/greek/sophist.txt

  start_marker: 228c
  end_marker: 228d
  start_char: 23348
  end_char: 24153
  text_sha256: ca71b4f4cef5e6146f5321e6dffe1d7746533860257f9a74575c78bb8b8c952b
greek_terms:
  - πονηρία
  - νόσος
  - ἄγνοιαν
  - κακίαν
english_gloss: wickedness, disease, ignorance, vice
observation: The Stranger identifies two kinds of badness in the soul at 228d. The first kind is called wickedness by the many and is most clearly a disease of the soul. The second kind they call ignorance, but when it occurs in the soul alone, they are unwilling to agree that it is a vice.
textual_basis: "At 228d the Stranger states that there are two kinds of bad things in the soul: one, called wickedness by the many, is clearly a disease of it. The other they call ignorance, but when it occurs in the soul alone they are unwilling to agree it is a vice. The Stranger thus registers a divergence between popular naming and what the argument requires."
limits: This observation records the naming divergence. It does not claim the Stranger endorses the popular view. It does not address whether the subsequent argument resolves the divergence.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_sophist_0068
source_work: Sophist
stephanus_span: 228e-229a
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/sophist.txt
  stephanus_span: 228e-229a
  start_marker: 228e
  end_marker: 229a
  start_char: 24153
  end_char: 24834
  text_sha256: 78a4336ba05addc403a4c0d9697e419c5a8020d989bbb31327bfed6f44894f29
greek_terms:
  - νόσον
  - αἶσχος
  - γυμναστική
  - ἰατρική
  - κολαστικὴ
  - διδασκαλικὴν
english_gloss: ""
observation: "The Eleatic Stranger pairs two bodily conditions and their crafts with two psychic conditions and their crafts: gymnastics addresses bodily ugliness just as the corrective art addresses hubris, injustice, and cowardice in the soul; medicine addresses bodily disease just as the teaching art addresses ignorance in the soul (228e-229a)."
textual_basis: At 228e Theaetetus agrees that psychic vice divides into disease (cowardice, licentiousness, injustice) and ugliness (extensive ignorance). The Stranger then asks whether two crafts arose for two bodily conditions (228e), identifies gymnastics for ugliness and medicine for disease (229a), and immediately maps the corrective art to the soul-disease triad and the teaching art to soul-ignorance (229a).
limits: Does not establish that the corrective art is itself a craft in the same sense as medicine or gymnastics; Theaetetus qualifies the claim about corrective art as merely according to human opinion.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_sophist_0069
source_work: Sophist
stephanus_span: 228e-229a
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/sophist.txt
  stephanus_span: 228e-229a
  start_marker: 228e
  end_marker: 229a
  start_char: 24153
  end_char: 24834
  text_sha256: 78a4336ba05addc403a4c0d9697e419c5a8020d989bbb31327bfed6f44894f29
greek_terms:
  - γένη
  - κακίας
  - νόσον
  - αἶσχος
english_gloss: ""
observation: "The Stranger and Theaetetus consolidate a dichotomous division of psychic vice: one genus is disease, containing cowardice, licentiousness, and injustice; the other genus is ugliness (deformity), consisting of extensive and varied ignorance (228e). Crafts are then assigned accordingly (229a)."
textual_basis: "Theaetetus at 228e concedes the earlier point that there are two genera of vice in the soul, and the terms are distributed: cowardice, licentiousness, and injustice together are to be considered disease in us, while the condition of much and varied ignorance is to be set down as ugliness (deformity)."
limits: This division of vice into two kinds is accepted for the sake of argument; it does not demonstrate that all vice reduces to these two genera or that the classification is exhaustive.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml

greek_terms:
  - εἰκός
  - ἀνθρωπίνην δόξαν
english_gloss: ""
observation: "Theaetetus gives a hedged assent when the Stranger claims that the corrective art is by nature most closely allied to Justice: he calls it merely likely, speaking according to human opinion (229a)."
textual_basis: "At 229a the Stranger claims the corrective art is most of all crafts naturally allied to Justice. Theaetetus replies with a qualification: 'likely, at any rate, to speak according to human opinion,' restricting the basis of the claim rather than offering full endorsement."
limits: The hedge does not indicate rejection; it signals that the claim rests on conventional or reputed grounds rather than demonstrated necessity. It does not address whether Theaetetus later gives unqualified assent to the parallel claim about the teaching art, which he does immediately afterward.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_sophist_0072
source_work: Sophist
stephanus_span: 229d-229e
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/sophist.txt
  stephanus_span: 229d-229e
  start_marker: 229d
  end_marker: 229e
  start_char: 25700
  end_char: 26347
  text_sha256: 2424e068d8d5c02ac40fdfdf427db8e28744e22fc5e284e773c1a3750368eba8
greek_terms:
  - δημιουργικὰς διδασκαλίας
  - παιδείαν
  - τραχυτέρα
  - λειότερον
  - ἀρχαιοπρεπές
english_gloss: ""
observation: |
  At 229d-229e the Eleatic Stranger divides instruction (didaschalias) into two kinds: craft-instruction (demiourgikas didaskalias) and education (paideian) understood as instruction in logoi. The latter (the instruction that operates through speeches) is then further divided using a road-metaphor: a rougher road (trachytera) versus a smoother road (leioteron). The rougher road is then identified by Theaetetus' question as an old-fashioned paternal admonition. The taxonomy is structured as a series of dichotomous branching decisions.
textual_basis: |
  The Stranger explicitly asks at 229d whether a further division (diairesin) exists, Theaetetus agrees to investigate, and the Stranger then articulates two branches at 229e: the rougher path and the smoother path within didaskalike in logoi. The pattern is a nested dichotomous division, with the first cut separating technical from discursive instruction and the second cut dividing the discursive instruction by a road-path metaphor.
limits: |
  This record does not assess whether the division is exhaustive or correct. It only notes the formal dichotomous pattern at this point in the taxonomy.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_sophist_0073
source_work: Sophist
stephanus_span: 229d
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/sophist.txt

  start_marker: 229d
  end_marker: 229d
  start_char: 25700
  end_char: 26077
  text_sha256: 7c80666b3d1c07154d52d7d2519f7e22f233cadb73eb9ac3cb963ad6696fb1c3
greek_terms:
  - σκεπτέον
  - διαίρεσιν
  - σκοπεῖν
english_gloss: ""
observation: |
  At 229d the Stranger proposes that the classification of instruction requires further examination and marks the next topic explicitly ("we must consider whether it is indivisible or admits a further split"). Theaetetus replies with the short assenting procedural formula "certainly we should look" (oukoun chre skopein). This constitutes a formal, stepwise agreement to continue the diairetic inquiry, with the Stranger setting the agenda and Theaetetus providing the explicit go-ahead before the next division is introduced.
textual_basis: |
  At 229d the Stranger marks the next step: "alla gar hemin eti kai touto skepteon, ar' atomon ede esti pan e tina echon diairesin axian eponymias." Theaetetus immediately assents with "oukoun chre skopein." Only after this procedural consent does the Stranger proceed to the next dichotomy.
limits: |
  The observation records the procedural pattern of agreement in this segment; it does not assess whether Theaetetus is merely conforming or whether the division is valid.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml

  text_sha256: e7701e2845ecb7a90256e69587723edaf30e0dd64b685d9be68cd72c45f59e4d
greek_terms:
  - ἀμαθία
  - τὸ μὴ κατειδότα τι δοκεῖν εἰδέναι
english_gloss: ignorance, folly, thinking one knows when one does not
observation: The Stranger defines a distinctive kind of ignorance (amathia) as thinking one knows something when one does not (229c). He identifies this as a great and difficult form (eidos) of ignorance, counterbalancing all other parts, and says it alone is properly called amathia.
textual_basis: At 229c the Stranger says he sees a great and difficult separated form of ignorance, then defines it as thinking one knows something when one does not, adding that all errors in thought arise through this. He then states this alone of ignorance is called amathia.
limits: This observation records the definition of amathia as a type of ignorance within the diairetic inquiry. It does not address whether the definition is adequate or how it relates to Socratic claims of ignorance elsewhere.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_sophist_0075
source_work: Sophist
stephanus_span: 229b-229c
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/sophist.txt

greek_terms:
  - διπλῆ
  - μόρια
english_gloss: cut, double, parts
observation: "The Stranger proposes that the division of ignorance into kinds determines the division of the teaching art: if ignorance has a cut down the middle and is double, then teaching must have two parts, one for each kind of ignorance (229b)."
textual_basis: "At 229b the Stranger says: if ignorance is double, clearly it compels the teaching art to have two parts, one for each of its kinds. This conforms to the diairetic method where a division in one domain maps onto a division in another."
limits: This observation records the structural mapping between ignorance-division and teaching-division. It does not claim this is the only instance of such mapping in the dialogue or that the division is exhaustive.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_sophist_0076
source_work: Sophist
stephanus_span: 230c-230d

  source_path: raw/plato/greek/sophist.txt

greek_terms:
  []
english_gloss: ""
observation: "The Eleatic Stranger compares the elenchus to medical purgation: just as doctors hold that a body cannot benefit from nourishment until internal obstructions are expelled, so too the soul cannot benefit from teachings until the elenchus removes opinions that block learning."
textual_basis: At 230c-d the Stranger explicitly maps the medical practice of purgation onto the elenctic process, using the analogy to explain why refutation must precede learning. The parallel is drawn with hosper hoi peri ta somata iatroi... t'auton kai peri psyches (just as doctors concerning bodies... the same also concerning the soul).
limits: The observation records the presence of the medical-purgation analogy, not whether the analogy is sustained or strained later in the dialogue. It does not claim the Stranger endorses this analogy without qualification.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
  - ἀγριώτατον ἡμερωτάτῳ
  - ὀλισθηρότατον γὰρ τὸ γένος
english_gloss: ""
observation: "The Stranger uses a proverb comparing the wolf to the dog to warn about deceptive resemblances: the sophist is most wild likened to the most tame, and the genus is most slippery."
textual_basis: At 231a the Stranger says the wolf is like the dog, the most wild to the most tame, and adds that one must guard against resemblances because the genus is most slippery. This functions as a craft-analogy caution against misidentifying the sophist's art.
limits: The observation notes the animal-comparison proverb as a methodological warning about likeness; it does not assert that the sophist is actually a wolf or reduce the definitional difficulty to the proverb alone.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
  - ὅμως δὲ ἔστω
  - περὶ σμικρῶν ὅρων
english_gloss: ""
observation: The Stranger provisionally accepts the identification of the sophist with the purgative practitioner despite his reservation, using the formula nevertheless let it be so, and indicates that the dispute concerns no small boundaries.
textual_basis: At 231a, after warning about deceptive resemblance, the Stranger says nevertheless let it stand, provisionally accepting the definition while signaling that it is contested. He adds that the dispute is not about small boundaries, framing the inquiry as procedurally open and high-stakes.
limits: The observation notes a procedural move to accept a provisional definition while flagging its contestability; it does not claim that this definition is final or that the sophist is captured by it.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_sophist_0080
source_work: Sophist
stephanus_span: 231b-231c
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/sophist.txt
  stephanus_span: 231b-231c
  start_marker: 231b
  end_marker: 231c
  start_char: 28717
  end_char: 29522
  text_sha256: ae3957c8c553cb49395c373a128a02f3528b51f40c3d0b274c6fedee4baabe16
greek_terms:
  - γένει γενναία σοφιστική
  - μάταιον δοξοσοφίαν
  - ἔλεγχος
english_gloss: ""
observation: At 231b the Stranger presents a seven-level nested diairetic division that terminates by identifying the elenchus as "noble sophistry by birth" (gènei gennaía sophistikḗ).
textual_basis: The chain runs discriminative art (diakritikḗ) → purificatory (kathartikḗ) → soul-purification → teaching (didaskalikḗ) → education (paideutikḗ) → elenchus concerning vain conceit-of-wisdom (mátaion doxosophían) → noble sophistry. The Stranger explicitly commands that this elenchus "be called nothing other than the noble sophistry by birth" at 231b.
limits: This observation identifies the nested structure and its terminus; it does not assess whether the identification of elenchus as noble sophistry is provisional or ironic, nor whether the Stranger later revises this classification.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml

greek_terms:
  - ἀπορῶ
  - ἐξαναπνεύσωμεν
english_gloss: ""
observation: At 231c, after completing a long diairetic chain, the Stranger calls for an explicit pause to catch breath (exanapneúsōmen) before continuing the hunt for the sophist.
textual_basis: Theaetetus declares aporia at 231b-231c because the sophist has appeared in many forms. The Stranger responds by saying the sophist is also in aporia, then issues the directive "first let us stand and catch our breath, so to speak" (prôton dḕ stántes hoîon exanapneúsōmen) at 231c, marking a deliberate mid-dialogue respite.
limits: This records a procedural pause embedded in the dialogue; it does not claim the pause signals a structural break in the argument or that the preceding definitions are abandoned.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_sophist_0082

  - τὰς ἁπάσας μὴ ῥᾴδιον εἶναι διαφεύγειν
english_gloss: ""
observation: At 231c the Stranger cites a proverb ("it is not easy to escape all of them") and applies it to the sophist's predicament of being surrounded by multiple argumentative captures.
textual_basis: The Stranger says the sophist must now be in great aporia about how he will slip through the argument, then introduces the proverb with "correct is the proverb" (orthḕ gàr hē paroimía) and applies it to the sophist's situation at 231c.
limits: This records the deployment of a proverb within dialectical context; it does not assess whether the proverb is original to Plato or traditional, nor whether its application is ironic.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_sophist_0083

greek_terms:
  - καθαρμός
  - διακρίσεως
english_gloss: ""
observation: The Eleatic Stranger assigns the name 'katharmos' (purification) to the kind of separation (diakrisis) that retains the better and discards the worse, while stating he has no name for the other kind that separates like from like.
textual_basis: "At 226d the Stranger explicitly says: 'Of the one I have no name spoken; but of the separation that retains the better and discards the worse, I do have one... all such separation is called a purification (katharmos) by everyone.' This is a terminological assignment within a branching distinction."
limits: The Stranger does not yet define purification or apply it to a domain; this is only the naming moment within a prior classification. The observation does not address whether this naming is provisional or final.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_sophist_0084
source_work: Sophist
stephanus_span: 226d-226e
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/sophist.txt
  stephanus_span: 226d-226e
  start_marker: 226d
  end_marker: 226e
  start_char: 20163
  end_char: 20850
  text_sha256: ff4fdb8d3012c78565313200cfe9b93e3345ae583b9d41072c028c99ce31532d
greek_terms:
  - καθαρτικὸν εἶδος
  - διπλοῦν
english_gloss: ""
observation: Immediately after naming purification, the Stranger announces that the purificative kind (kathartikon eidos) is double (diploun), initiating a further division.
textual_basis: "At 226d-226e the Stranger asks: 'Would not everyone see that the purificative kind, being double, is twofold?' Theaetetus responds that he does not yet see it clearly, but the Stranger proceeds to subdivide bodily purifications."
limits: The double structure is announced but not yet fully elaborated within this span. The observation does not assess whether the double division is exhaustive.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_sophist_0085
source_work: Sophist
stephanus_span: 226d-226e

  end_char: 20850
  text_sha256: ff4fdb8d3012c78565313200cfe9b93e3345ae583b9d41072c028c99ce31532d
greek_terms:
  - οὐ μὴν ἔγωγε καθορῶ νῦν
english_gloss: ""
observation: When the Stranger announces the purificative kind is double, Theaetetus responds that he does not yet see it clearly (ou men egoge kathoro nyn), deferring to the Stranger for further exposition.
textual_basis: "At 226e Theaetetus says: 'Yes, at leisure perhaps; but I for my part do not yet see clearly now.' This is a standard respondent move in the elenctic-dialectical sequence where the interlocutor signals that further guidance is needed before assent can be given."
limits: This is a single moment of non-comprehension, not a pattern of collapse or refutation. The observation does not claim Theaetetus is being ironic or resistant.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
english_gloss: the antilogic art
observation: |
  The Eleatic Stranger introduces the antilogic art as the next topic for
  investigation, framing it as a power sufficient for disputation about
  all things.
textual_basis: |
  At 232e the Stranger asks whether the antilogic art appears to be some
  sufficient power for dispute about all things and Theaetetus assents.
limits: |
  The passage marks the antilogic art as a target for definition; it does
  not yet define it or distinguish it from sophistry.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation: |
  The Stranger defers to Theaetetus's youth, suggesting that the young may
  see the question more sharply while the older see it more dimly.
textual_basis: |
  At 232e the Stranger says that perhaps the young would look toward it
  more sharply, but we (the older) more dimly.
limits: |
  The remark deploys a conventional age trope; it does not itself
  establish whether the Stranger genuinely defers.
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_sophist_0088
source_work: Sophist
stephanus_span: 232a-232b
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/sophist.txt
  stephanus_span: 232a-232b
  start_marker: 232a
  end_marker: 232b
  start_char: 30225
  end_char: 31039
  text_sha256: 0f37b1da11154adda5302948cbfab7c70ff5e1080455a08c4c0b3fe2e0308a9d
greek_terms:
  - πολλῶν
  - μιᾶς δὲ τέχνης
  - φάντασμα
english_gloss: appearance of polymathy under one craft name not sound; inability to see the single unifying object of the craft
observation: "The Eleatic Visitor states a diagnostic criterion for distinguishing genuine craft mastery from the mere appearance of polymathy: when someone appears knowledgeable about many things but is called by the name of a single craft, this appearance is unsound; the person cannot see that one thing in the craft toward which all its learnings are directed."
textual_basis: At 232a the Visitor says the appearance of polymathy under one craft name is not sound, and the cause is inability to discern the single unifying object of that craft.
limits: This observation records the articulation of a craft-unity criterion at 232a; it does not assess whether the criterion is later applied or whether it adequately distinguishes the sophist from genuine practitioners.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml

greek_terms:
  - ἀναλάβωμεν
  - ἐξ ἀρχῆς
english_gloss: let us take up, skilled in contradiction, from the beginning
observation: The Eleatic Visitor recapitulates a previously established characterization of the sophist — that he is antilogikos — and proposes a fresh examination from the beginning about what the sophist makes others antilogikoi about.
textual_basis: At 232b the Visitor proposes to take up one thing first from what has been said about the sophist, identifies it as the antilogikos characterization, and then announces a new examination from the beginning.
limits: This observation records the structural move of recapitulation followed by a new inquiry start; it does not evaluate whether the new starting point is adequate.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_sophist_0090
source_work: Sophist
stephanus_span: 230a-230b
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/sophist.txt
  stephanus_span: 230a-230b
  start_marker: 230a
  end_marker: 230b
  start_char: 26347
  end_char: 27219
  text_sha256: a7c06de6b47806c0753c2e8511297b07f5975b7cf407a6a2da55f12312a47fc0
greek_terms:
  - διερωτῶσιν
  - πλανωμένων τὰς δόξας
  - συνάγοντες
  - ἐναντίας
english_gloss: ""
observation: |
  The Eleatic Stranger describes the elenctic method in procedural detail: practitioners question someone who thinks they are saying something meaningful but is saying nothing, examine their wandering opinions, collect them in speech, set them side by side, and demonstrate that they contradict themselves about the same things in the same respects.
textual_basis: |
  At 230b the Stranger answers Theaetetus's question about the method of expelling false opinion: "They question someone about things where the person thinks they're saying something but is saying nothing; then, since their opinions wander, they easily examine them, and gathering them together in speech, they place them side by side, and having placed them, they demonstrate that they are contrary to themselves about the same things, in relation to the same things, in the same respects."
limits: |
  The passage describes the elenctic procedure in the third person as a reported method; it does not show the Stranger or Theaetetus performing or undergoing elenchus within this segment.
review_status: rejected
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_sophist_0091
source_work: Sophist
stephanus_span: 230a-230b
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/sophist.txt
  stephanus_span: 230a-230b
  start_marker: 230a
  end_marker: 230b
  start_char: 26347
  end_char: 27219
  text_sha256: a7c06de6b47806c0753c2e8511297b07f5975b7cf407a6a2da55f12312a47fc0
greek_terms:
  - πᾶσαν ἀκούσιον ἀμαθίαν
english_gloss: ""
observation: |
  The Eleatic Stranger reports that some practitioners hold that all ignorance is involuntary, and that this belief motivates their preference for refutation over admonitory education.
textual_basis: |
  At 230a the Stranger says: "Some, having given an account to themselves, have come to believe that all ignorance is involuntary, and that someone who thinks they are wise would never want to learn about things they think they're clever about, and that the admonitory kind of education accomplishes little with much effort."
limits: |
  The Stranger attributes this view to others rather than asserting it in his own voice. The observation records a reported premise for the elenctic method, not an argument for the truth of the claim that ignorance is involuntary.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_sophist_0092

  - ἑαυτοῖς μὲν χαλεπαίνουσι
  - πρὸς δὲ τοὺς ἄλλους ἡμεροῦνται
english_gloss: ""
observation: |
  The Stranger reports that those subjected to elenctic refutation, upon seeing their own opinions demonstrated to be contradictory, become angry at themselves but gentle toward others.
textual_basis: |
  At 230b the Stranger says of those who witness their contradictory opinions laid side by side: "those observing this become angry at themselves but gentle toward others."
limits: |
  This is a reported effect described in general terms by the Stranger, not a dramatic enactment within this segment. The observation does not address whether this effect is always produced or universally desirable.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_sophist_0093
source_work: Sophist
stephanus_span: 234e-235a
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/sophist.txt

  end_char: 36544
  text_sha256: 32e9828d0133bbb6facea9dc0da597e7fb68d22dae808c65f63f72cfb436c0d4
greek_terms:
  - γόητα
  - μιμητὴν
english_gloss: sorcerer, imitator
observation: The Eleatic Stranger proposes that the sophist be classified as a sorcerer and imitator, and Theaetetus assents without qualification at 234e-235a.
textual_basis: At 234e-235a the Stranger asks whether it is now clear that the sophist is one of the sorcerers, being an imitator of the things that are, and then declares that he must be posited as a sorcerer and imitator, to which Theaetetus replies 'how could he not be so posited?'.
limits: This is a provisional classification offered before the division of image-making is introduced; it does not constitute the dialogue's final definition of the sophist.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_sophist_0094
source_work: Sophist

greek_terms:
  - παιδιᾶς
  - σχεδὸν ἤδη σαφὲς
english_gloss: play/child's-play, nearly now clear
observation: Theaetetus hedges his assent at 235a with the phrase 'nearly now clear,' qualifying the certainty of the sophist's classification even as he agrees with it.
textual_basis: When the Stranger asks whether the classification is clear, Theaetetus replies that it is nearly now clear from what has been said, inserting the hedge 'nearly' before affirming clarity.
limits: This observation registers the hedging language but does not interpret Theaetetus's motive or claim that the hedge undermines the definition.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_sophist_0095
source_work: Sophist
stephanus_span: 235a
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/sophist.txt

  text_sha256: dcf0c72f1520a9033cfe53874be2a75ab0d34cdef68622e463043a57d161e131
greek_terms:
  - τῶν τῆς παιδιᾶς μετεχόντων
english_gloss: those who partake in play
observation: Theaetetus characterizes the sophist as belonging to those who partake in play at 235a, framing sophistic activity as non-serious rather than as genuine expertise.
textual_basis: Theaetetus says the sophist is one of the classes partaking in play. The term for play contrasts with serious activity and classifies the sophist's enterprise as a kind of game rather than a craft producing knowledge.
limits: This does not establish that the term for play is a technical term of Platonic criticism; it registers only that Theaetetus uses the vocabulary of play to characterize the sophist at this juncture.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_sophist_0096
source_work: Sophist
stephanus_span: 234a-234b
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/sophist.txt
  stephanus_span: 234a-234b
  start_marker: 234a
  end_marker: 234b
  start_char: 34062
  end_char: 34987
  text_sha256: 438dfb2626a1ff61be872c9fe58bdaa399b589cce84ea32fdab6c7261307bcbb
greek_terms:
  - μιμητικόν
english_gloss: play, imitative
observation: Theaetetus calls the sophist's claimed universal production a kind of play, and the Eleatic Stranger asks whether the promise to know and teach everything against a small fee must also be considered play. The Stranger then frames the entire mimetic kind as a species of play.
textual_basis: At 234a Theaetetus responds that the Stranger speaks of a kind of play to the Stranger's description; the Stranger extends the term at 234a-234b, asking whether it must be considered play, and then asking whether any species of play is more artful or graceful than the mimetic kind.
limits: The observation records the classification of sophistry under play and mimesis. It does not establish whether the Stranger endorses this classification as final or is developing a provisional frame.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_sophist_0097
source_work: Sophist
stephanus_span: 234a-234b
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/sophist.txt
  stephanus_span: 234a-234b
  start_marker: 234a
  end_marker: 234b
  start_char: 34062
  end_char: 34987
  text_sha256: 438dfb2626a1ff61be872c9fe58bdaa399b589cce84ea32fdab6c7261307bcbb
greek_terms:
  - μιμήματα
  - ὁμώνυμα
  - γραφικῇ τέχνῃ
english_gloss: imitations, homonymous, graphic art
observation: "The Eleatic Stranger characterizes the sophist's universal production claim through an analogy to graphic art: the sophist produces imitations and homonyms of real beings and deceives the unthinking by displaying them from a distance, as a painter displays painted things."
textual_basis: At 234b the Stranger states that the person promising to make all things by one craft produces imitations and homonyms of real beings through the graphic art, and can escape the notice of thoughtless young people by displaying painted things from afar.
limits: The observation identifies the graphic-art analogy for mimesis and appearance/reality distinction. It does not evaluate whether this analogy adequately captures the sophist's actual practice or whether the Stranger will refine it.
review_status: accepted
```
